                                                                                                               Frost Design for Roads

       Notes.
       1. The function of the subbase as a filter layer is not always required, depending upon the subgrade
       material. In this case, the subgrade is a CL; therefore, it is required.
       2. For economy, the thickness of the base and subbase courses can be adjusted, so long as the
       minimum thickness above the CBR = 15 subbase is maintained at 4 inches, as determined from
       Figure F-2, page F-4.
       3. An overall minimal thickness layer of 4 inches should be maintained.

F-14. When using a geotextile as a filter layer, the design could be reduced by deducting 6 inches of the clean
sand subbase and replacing it with a geotextile. The total thickness above the geotextile must be a minimum
of 25 inches. Two alternate designs using geotextile are shown in figure F-3.

                                                Appendix G

Airfield Cone Penetrometer and Dual-Mass Dynamic Cone
                                Penetrometer

         Two 12 5/8-inch extensions are graduated every 2 inches, and two 11/32-inch wrenches
         are used to tighten the set in during assembly.

AIRFIELD CONE PENETROMETER

     G-1. The airfield cone penetrometer comes with a carrying case that is 14 3/4 inches long and houses the
     cone unit and a handle that screws off the base. Two 12 5/8-inch extensions are graduated every 2 inches,
     and two 11/32-inch wrenches are used to tighten the set in assembly. An extra cone and pin for the handle
     are also included.

     G-2. The airfield cone penetrometer is constructed of durable metals and needs little care other than cleaning
     and oiling. The calibration should be checked occasionally. The load indicator should read 0 when the
     instrument is suspended by the handle and 15 when a 150-pound load is placed on the handle. If an error of
     more than 5 percent is noted, the penetrometer should be recalibrated.

     G-3. The airfield cone penetrometer must not be confused with the trafficability penetrometer, which is a
     standard military item included in the soil test set. If the trafficability penetrometer is used to measure the
     AI, the reading obtained with the 0.2-square inch cone must be divided by 20, and the readings obtained with
     the 0.5-square-inch cone must be divided by 50.

     G-4. The airfield cone penetrometer does well when used in silt or clay. The readings are meaningless in
     gravel, so the AI is determined for the soil beneath the gravel layer.

     G-5. Sands require special treatment. Many sands occur in a loose state. When very dry sands show
     increasing AIs with depth, the 2-inch depth index is often low (about AI 3 or AI 4). Sands can usually support
     aircraft with requirements much higher than AI 3 or AI 4 because the strength of the sand increases under
     the confining action of aircraft tires. Generally, dry sand or gravel is adequate for aircraft in the C-130 class,
     regardless of the penetrometer readings. Avoid sands and gravel in a quick condition (water percolating
     through them). The evaluation of moist sands should be determined by the same method as the one used for
     fine soils.

     G-6. Because soil conditions are immediately and significantly affected by weather, an evaluation is valid
     only for the period immediately after measurements are made. However, the evaluation will remain constant
     as long as the soil moisture content does not change.

ASSEMBLY AND OPERATION OF THE AIRFIELD PENETROMETER

     G-7. To assemble an airfield penetrometer, detach the handle and cone from the housing, attach the staff
     extension to the housing, and attach the cone to the staff extension. Insert the handle to the top of the housing,
     and tighten the extension using an 11/32-inch wrench. Do not over-tighten the extension. Excessive force
     can bend or break shafts.

     G-8. To operate the airfield penetrometer, inspect the device for tightness. Ensure that the handle, cone, and
     extension are properly attached. Next, position your hands vertically on the handle, and place the
     penetrometer vertically on the soil surface. Slowly apply force at a rate of 1/2 inch to 1 inch per second, and
     penetrate the soil. Take readings at 2-inch increments, up to 24 inches or until a maximum AI (15) is obtained.
     Discard the 0-inch reading. Readings should be recorded in tabular fashion, similar to the sample format
     shown in figure G-1, page G-2. If you suspect that the cone is encountering stone or other foreign body at
     the depth at which a reading is desired, make another penetration nearby.

29 February 2016  TM 3-34.48-2  G-1

     G-9. Use an X configuration in a 12-inch radius circle to take five penetrations at each location. Note the
     readings at each 2-inch increment. When an AI (15) has been reached, lower depths can be assigned an AI
     (15), provided that a minimum of three sites have been tested to ensure that the lower depth has an AI (15)
     or higher. Tests should be taken at least every 6 inches down to the 24-inch depth or lower for the heaviest
     C-17 landing and highest pass levels. If very low AI (4) values appear to exist below the 24-inch depths, an
     auger may be used to penetrate to the desired depth.

     G-10. If a low strength layer is present, enough readings must be taken at full depth (24 inches) to verify the
     extent of the area containing the layer. Since this is the most critical area, the entire airfield must be evaluated
     based on the abnormal layer.

     G-11. After the readings at a site have been taken, the readings for each depth at the site are averaged. The
     critical depth is then determined, and the critical AI is determined by averaging the average readings in the
     critical depth. After critical AIs are determined for the sites, the lowest critical AI is assigned for the entire
     airfield. The AI can then be used to estimate the CBR using figure G-2. Surface thickness requirements
     should be determined as described in chapter 4.

 CBR California Bearing Ratio

                                    Figure G-2. Correlation between AI and CBR

DETERMINATION OF CRITICAL AIRFIELD INDEX

     G-12. A minimum of one reading at the parking apron, runway, and hammerhead must be taken to the 24-
     inch depth to ensure that the soil profile is accurate. If a soft layer is suspected or located beneath a hard,
     upper layer, take enough readings down to the 24-inch depth to ensure complete and accurate coverage of
     the area.

RUNWAYS AND TAXIWAYS

     G-13. The first reading should be taken 50 feet from one end of the runway or taxiway on the centerline.
     Additional readings should be obtained every 200 feet, staggered 20 feet off the centerline. This means that
     if the second reading is 20 feet right of the centerline, the third reading will be 20 feet left of the centerline,
     and so on. This pattern should be repeated until the midpoint is reached. After the midpoint, the survey team
     should go to the other end of the runway or taxiway, start the readings 50 feet from that end, and repeat the
     survey pattern back to the midpoint (see figure G-3, page G-4).

29 February 2016  TM 3-34.48-2  G-3


---

Appendix G

PARKING APRONS

     G-14. The first set of readings will be obtained at the center of the apron. Additional readings should be taken
     every 200 feet in both directions as shown in figure G-3. This should be repeated until the extent of the area
     has been covered.

                  Figure G-3. Survey pattern for runways, taxiways, and parking aprons

HAMMERHEAD TURNAROUNDS

     G-15. The initial set of readings should be obtained at the center of the turnaround. Additional readings
     should be taken 40 feet in both directions until the extent of the area has been covered (see figure G-4).

DUAL-MASS DYNAMIC CONE PENETROMETER DEVICE

     G-16. The dual-mass DCP consists of a 5/8-inch diameter steel rod with a steel cone attached to one end,
     which is driven into the pavement or subgrade by a sliding dual-mass hammer (see figure G-5, page G-6).
     The angle of the cone is 60 degrees, and the diameter of the base of the cone is 0.79 inches. The cone is
     hardened to increase service life. The diameter of the cone is 0.16 inch larger than that of the rod to ensure
     that penetration resistance is exerted on the cone. Figure G-6, page G-7, shows an assembled DCP with a
     vertical scale for measuring cone penetration depth. The DCP is driven into the soil by dropping a 17.6-pound
     or 10.1-pound sliding hammer from a height of 22.6 inches. The 17.6-pound hammer is converted to
     10.1 pounds by removing the hexagonal setscrew and the outer steel sleeve (see figure G-7, page G-7). This
     procedure can be accomplished during a test because the outer steel sleeve is designed to slide over the DCP
     handle. The cone penetration caused by one blow of the 17.6-pound hammer is twice the blow caused by one
     blow of the 10.l-pound hammer. The 10.1-pound hammer is more suitable for use and yields better test results
     in weaker soils having a CBR value of 10 or less. The 17.6-pound hammer penetrates high-strength soils
     quicker and may be preferred when these soils are encountered. The depth of cone penetration is measured
     at selected penetration or hammer-drop intervals, and the soil shear strength is reported in terms of DCP
     index. The DCP index is based on the average penetration depth resulting from one blow of the 17.6-pound
     hammer. The average penetration per hammer blow of the 10.l-pound hammer must be multiplied by two in

29 February 2016  TM 3-34.48-2  G-5


---

Appendix G

     order to obtain the DCP index value. The DCP is designed to penetrate soils to depths of 36 inches. Individual
     DCP index values are reported for each test depth, resulting in a soil-strength-with-depth profile for each test
     location.

DUAL-MASS DYNAMIC CONE PENETROMETER KIT

     engineer use. The kit includes—

             Three hardened cone adapters and 200 disposable cones.

DISPOSABLE CONE

     G-18. The disposable cone is for use in soils where a standard cone is difficult to remove. The disposable
     cone mounts on an adapter and is shown in figure G-9. At the conclusion of the test, the disposable cone
     easily slides off the cone adapter, allowing the operator to easily remove the DCP device from the soil. The
     disposable cone remains in the soil. Use of the disposable cone approximately doubles the number of tests
     per day that can be run by two operators.

GO/NO GO GAUGE

     G-19. The go/no go gauge is used to ensure that the base diameter of the cone is within proper tolerance.
     Each new fixed cone should be checked before use and at selected usage intervals to ensure that the base
     diameter of the cone is within a proper tolerance of between 0.78 and 0.8 inch. The cone must be replaced if
     its base diameter fits into both ends or neither end of the go/no go gauge. The cone is within proper tolerance
     when it fits into only one end of the gauge.

G-8         TM 3-34.48-2                                              29 February 2016


---

                  Airfield Cone Penetrometer and Dual-Mass Dynamic Cone Penetrometer

                                      Figure G-9. Disposable cone and adaptor

USE OF THE DYNAMIC CONE PENETROMETER

     G-20. The DCP test causes wear to the metal parts of the DCP device. Parts of the DCP device will eventually
     suffer fatigue failure and will have to be repaired or replaced. In order to ensure maximum service life, the
     DCP should be inspected before it is used to ensure that the joints are tight. A thread-locking compound
     should be used on loose joints. Also, the base diameter of the cone should be checked to ensure that it is
     within tolerance. If the cone point becomes bent or too blunt to penetrate around aggregate, it must be
     replaced.

     G-21. Two people (an operator and a recorder) are required to operate the DCP. The operator holds the device
     by its handle in a vertical position and taps the device using the hammer until the base of the cone is flush
     with the surface of the soil. The recorder then checks the device for a zero reading by holding the vertical
     scale between the soil surface and the bottom of the hammer. The bottom of the 4-inch-diameter portion of
     the hammer should read 0 millimeters on the vertical scale. If the hammer is above the vertical scale, the
     hammer should be tapped until it is flush with 0 millimeters on the vertical scale. In weak soils, the weight
     of the DCP device will sink the cone past its zero reading. In this case, a zero-blow penetration reading is
     recorded, in millimeters, at the actual measured pretest depth. The hammer is then raised to the bottom of the
     handle and dropped. Care should be exercised when raising the hammer to ensure that the hammer is touching
     the bottom of the handle but not lifting the cone before it is allowed to drop. The hammer must be allowed
     to fall freely, with its downward movement not influenced by hand movements. The operator should also be
     careful not to exert a downward force on the handle after dropping the hammer. The recorder is responsible
     for recording the number of hammer blows between measurements. He is also responsible for measuring and
     recording the penetration after each set of hammer blows. Penetration measurements are recorded to the
     nearest 5 millimeters. As an example of how to read the penetration depth, figure G-10, page G-10, shows a
     penetration depth of 150 millimeters.

     G-22. The cone must penetrate a minimum of 25 millimeters between recorded measurements. Data taken at
     less than 25-millimeter penetration increments are unnecessary and sometimes result in inaccurate strength
     determinations. The number of hammer blows between measurement recordings will typically be 20, 10, 5,
     3, 2, or 1, depending on the soil strength and the cone penetration rate. This should be selected so that average
     penetrations of 25 to 35 millimeters are achieved for recording. The operator and recorder should be alert to
     sudden increases in the cone-penetration rates during the test. A noticeable increase in the penetration rate
     indicates a weaker soil layer. The operator should stop and allow the recorder to record the blow count and
     penetration depth whenever a weaker soil layer is encountered.

     G-23. After the cone has been driven to the desired test depth (maximum 39 inches), it is extracted from the
     soil by driving the hammer against the top handle. Caution must be exercised during this operation to prevent
     damage to the DCP device. The hammer must be raised in a vertical direction (rather than in an arcing
     motion), or the rod may be bent or broken at the connection to the anvil. In soils where great difficulty is

29 February 2016  TM 3-34.48-2  G-9


---

Appendix G

     encountered in extracting the DCP device, disposable cones should be used. Using disposable cones will save
     wear and tear on the device and the operator. In soils with large aggregate, the DCP may try to penetrate the
     soil at a slant rather than from a vertical direction. The operator should not apply force to the handle of the
     DCP in an attempt to force it to penetrate the soil vertically. Lateral force on the handle in an attempt to make
     the DCP penetrate the soil vertically will cause the upper handle rod to fatigue and break at the point where
     it screws into the anvil. Instead, the test should be stopped when the handle deviates laterally 6 inches or
     more from the vertical position, and a new test should be attempted at another location.

       Figure G-10. Example penetration measurement showing 150-millimeter penetration

DYNAMIC CONE PENETROMETER MAINTENANCE

     G-24. The DCP should be kept clean, and soil should be removed from the penetration rod and cone after
     each test. A light application of spray lubricant or oil should be applied to the hammer slide rod before each
     daily use. Loose joints will lead to equipment failure, so joints should be constantly monitored and kept tight.
     Problem joints should be treated with a joint-locking compound. The lower penetration rod should be kept
     clean and lubricated with oil when clay soils are tested.

SOIL STRENGTH EVALUATIONS USING THE DYNAMIC CONE
PENETROMETER

     G-25. Using the DCP requires a variety of measurements at different locations and depths depending on the
     project. This provides data to correlate with the CBR and tabulate for further analysis.

G-10  TM 3-34.48-2  29 February 2016


---

                                               Airfield Cone Penetrometer and Dual-Mass Dynamic Cone Penetrometer

NUMBER OF MEASUREMENTS

     G-26. The number of measurements to be made, the location of the measurements, the depth of the measure-
     ments required, and the frequency of recording data with depth vary with the type of road or airfield pavement
     operation and with the time available for conducting the tests. For this reason, hard-and-fast rules for the
     number of tests required in evaluating roads and airfields are impracticable. Soil conditions are extremely
     variable. The strength range and uniformity of soils or existing pavement materials will generally control the
     number of measurements necessary. In all cases, it is advisable to first test those spots that appear to be the
     weakest. Since the weakest condition controls the pavement evaluation, penetrations in areas that appear to
     be firm and uniform may be few and widely spaced. In areas of doubtful strength, penetration tests should be
     more closely spaced. No less than three penetration tests should be made in each area having similar soil
     conditions.

READING DEPTHS IN SOIL

     G-27. Soil strength usually increases with depth, but in some cases a thin, hard crust will overlay a soft layer,
     or the soil will contain thin layers of hard and soft material. For this reason and the fact that many aircraft
     and some military vehicles will affect the soil to depths of 36 inches or more, it is recommended that each
     penetration be made to a depth of 36 inches unless prevented by a very hard condition at a lesser depth. Soil
     test depths may be reduced when required traffic operations are known and the thickness requirements
     indicate that a reduced thickness above the subgrade controls the evaluation.

CORRELATION OF DYNAMIC CONE PENETROMETER INDEX WITH CALIFORNIA BEARING
RATIO

     G-28. A correlation of DCP index with CBR is necessary because the CBR is the soil strength value used for
     designing and evaluating unsurfaced, aggregate-surfaced, and flexible pavements for military roads and
     airfields. A database of field CBR versus DCP index values was collected from many sites and different soil
     types. The data showed that a separate correlation was required for CH soils and for wet CL soils with a CBR
     below 10 (see figure G-11). Table G-1, page G-12, shows a tabulated correlation of DCP index with CBR
     for the soil conditions.

     G-29. The data can be tabulated in spreadsheet format during testing. The number of hammer blows, the
     hammer weight, and the cone penetration (to the nearest 5 millimeters) need to be recorded. Figure G-12
     shows a sample format for a DCP data sheet.

EXCEL SPREADSHEET FOR DATA ANALYSIS

     G-30. Figure G-13, page G-14, shows a printout from the Microsoft Excel™ spreadsheet program developed
     by ERDC for use in analyzing DCP test data. The Excel DCP program is included in the DCP kit and is also
     available from ERDC or the DCP manufacturer. The DCP data shown was obtained on a test road consisting
     of 10 inches of crushed gravel over a very weak CH subgrade. A geogrid over geotextile was installed over
     the soft subgrade to separate and reinforce the aggregate layer. The CBR versus depth plot in figure G-13
     shows a 10-inch layer of base material with an 80 to 100 CBR over a very weak subgrade soil having a CBR
     of 0.6 to 0.7. The stair-step increase in subgrade strength below the 15-inch depth is typical in plastic clay
     soils. This occurs due to the plastic clay sticking to the DCP penetration rod at test depths below 12 inches.
     The strength of the CH soil may not actually be increasing as indicated at the 17-inch depth. The
     geogrid/geotextile layer was easily located by the DCP as it took several hammer drops to penetrate the
     geotextile at the 10-inch depth (See figure G-12).

APPLICATION OF DYNAMIC CONE PENETROMETER DATA

     G-31. UFC 3-250-09FA can be used for evaluating the potential of military operations on unsurfaced soils
     and aggregate-surfaced roads and airfields based on existing soil conditions. The evaluation procedure is the
     reverse of the design procedure. CBR and thickness evaluation data from the DCP tests are used to enter the
     appropriate set of design curves to determine the allowable design index for roads or the allowable gross

G-14                                   TM 3-34.48-2                          29 February 2016


---

                                               Airfield Cone Penetrometer and Dual-Mass Dynamic Cone Penetrometer

     weight and aircraft pass configuration for airfields. The design index for roads is then used to determine the
     allowable road classification and number of vehicle passes per day for various traffic categories.

     G-32. For unsurfaced soils in which the soil strength increases with depth, the average strength of the top
     layer is first used to ensure that compaction to a higher strength or the addition of a surfacing aggregate layer
     is not required. If the top layer of soil is adequate to support the desired design index or aircraft passes, then
     the strength of weaker soil layers beneath the top layer is used in order to check for adequate thickness
     requirements of the surfacing layers of soil.

     G-33. For aggregate-surfaced roads and airfields, the subgrade soil strength and aggregate layer strength
     should be used to ensure that the aggregate thickness and strength requirements are adequate for a given
     design index or aircraft pass level.

SPECIAL CONSIDERATIONS

     G-34. DCP operators must consider other conditions that could influence the data collected. Weather, soil
     type, or failure of the DCP to penetrate have different effects on the DCP index, CBR values, or trafficability
     of the soil.

WEATHER

     G-35. Because soil conditions are immediately and significantly affected by weather, an evaluation is valid
     only for the period immediately after measurements are made for unsurfaced pavements. However, it can
     usually be assumed that the evaluation will remain constant as long as no rain occurs. Gravel-surfaced
     pavements will be affected to a much lesser extent by rain.

CLAY SOILS

     G-36. DCP tests in highly plastic clays are generally accurate for depths to approximately 12 inches. At
     deeper depths, clay sticking to the lower rod may indicate higher CBR values than the actual values. Oiling
     the penetration rod will help prevent the clay from sticking to the penetration rod; however, it will not
     significantly improve the test results. A 2-inch-diameter (or larger) auger can be used to open the test hole
     up after each 12-inch DCP test penetration. This will eliminate clay and lower-rod friction problems and
     allow the test to accurately measure the clay soil strength for an additional 12 inches.

SANDS

     G-37. Many sands occur in a loose state. When relatively dry, loose sands show no DCP index values for the
     top few inches and may show increasing DCP index values with depth. The confining action of aircraft tires
     will increase the strength of the sand. Generally, dry sand or gravel will be adequate for aircraft in the C-130
     class, regardless of the DCP index values. Sands and gravels in a quick condition (water percolating through
     them) must be avoided. Evaluation of moist sands should be based on the DCP tests as described above.

SOIL REMOLDING

     G-38. Soil remolding is the changing or working of a soil by traffic. The effects of traffic remolding may
     have a beneficial, neutral, or detrimental effect and result in a change of soil strength. Additional DCP tests
     should be run after some traffic has been applied to determine any changes that may have occurred in soil
     strengths.

CONE-PENETRATION REFUSAL

     G-39. If the cone does not penetrate 25 millimeters after 10 blows with the 17.6-pound hammer (20 blows
     with the 10.1-pound hammer), the test should be stopped. If this firm material is a stabilized soil or high-
     strength aggregate base layer, it should be cored or drilled with an auger to allow access of the DCP cone to
     underlying layers. The DCP test can then proceed through the access hole after the depth of the material layer
     has been recorded. The material layer is assigned a CBR value of 100+. However, if a core or auger drill is
     not available, the 17.6-pound DCP hammer can normally be used to drive the lower rod and cone through

29 February 2016  TM 3-34.48-2  G-15


---

Appendix G

     the firm material. If a large rock or other object stopped the cone penetration, the DCP should be extracted
     and another attempt made within a few feet of the initial test. The DCP is generally not suitable for soils
     having significant amounts of aggregate greater than a 2-inch sieve size.

G-16  TM 3-34.48-2  29 February 2016


---

                                                Appendix H

                       Airfield Cone Penetrometer

         The airfield cone penetrometer is a probe instrument that provides an index of soil
         strength. It uses a 30-degree, right circular cone with a base diameter of 1/2 inch and
         an indicator that gives a reading directly in terms of an AI. The AI can then be used to
         estimate a CBR value, as shown in figure H-1, page H-2. This correlation has been
         established to yield values of CBR that generally are conservative. The tendency
         towards conservatism is necessary because there is no unique relationship between
         these measurements and a wide range of soil types. The curve should not be used to
         estimate AI values from the CBR determination because these generally would not be
         conservative.

BASIC SET

     H-1. The airfield cone penetrometer must not be confused with the trafficability penetrometer, which is a
     standard military item included in the soil test set. If the trafficability penetrometer is used to measure the
     AI, the reading obtained with the 0.2-square inch cone must be divided by 20, and the readings obtained with
     the 0.5-square-inch cone must be divided by 50.

     H-2. The airfield cone penetrometer comes with a carrying case that is 14 3/4 inches long and houses the
     cone unit and a handle that screws off the base. Two 12 5/8-inch extensions are graduated every 2 inches,
     and two 11/32-inch wrenches are used to tighten the set in assembly. An extra cone and an extra pin for the
     handle are also included.

     H-3. The airfield cone penetrometer is constructed of durable metals and needs little care other than cleaning
     and oiling. The calibration should be checked occasionally. The load indicator should read 0 when the
     instrument is suspended by the handle and 15 when a 150-pound load is placed on the handle. If an error of
     more than 5 percent is noted, the penetrometer should be recalibrated.

     H-4. The airfield cone penetrometer does well when used in silt or clay. In gravel the readings are
     meaningless, and the AI is determined for the soil beneath the gravel layer.

     H-5. Sands require special treatment. Many sands occur in a loose state. When very dry sands show
     increasing AIs with depth, but the 2-inch depth index is often low—about AI 3 or AI 4. Sands can usually
     support aircraft with requirements much higher than AI 3 or AI 4 because the strength of the sand increases
     under the confining action of the aircraft tires. Generally, dry sand or gravel is adequate for aircraft in the
     C-130 class, regardless of the penetrometer readings. Avoid sands and gravel in a quick condition (water
     percolating through them). Evaluation of moist sands should be determined by the same method as used for
     a fine soil.

29 February 2016  TM 3-34.48-2  H-1


---

Appendix H

     Legend:  California Bearing Ratio
     CBR                                  Figure H-1. Correlation of CBR and AI

     H-6. Because soil conditions are immediately and significantly affected by weather, an evaluation is valid
     only for the period immediately after measurements are made. However, the evaluation will remain constant
     as long as the soil moisture content does not change.

ASSEMBLY AND OPERATION OF AN AIRFIELD PENETROMETER

     H-7. To assemble an airfield penetrometer, detach the handle and cone from the housing, attach the staff
     extension to the housing, and attach the cone to the staff extension. Insert the handle to the top of the housing
     and tighten the extension using an 11/32-inch wrench. Be careful not to overtighten because excessive force
     can bend or break the shafts.

     H-8. Use the following procedures to operate an airfield penetrometer:
              Step 1. Inspect for tightness and ensure that the handle, cone, and extension are properly attached.
              Step 2. Place the penetrometer in a vertical position on the soil, and place your hands in a vertical
                  position on the handle.
              Step 3. Slowly apply force at a rate of 1/2 inch to 1 inch per second and penetrate the soil. Take
                  readings at 2-inch increments, up to 24 inches or until a maximum AI (15) is obtained. Discard

H-2           TM 3-34.48-2                                                       29 February 2016


---

                                                                                                                Airfield Cone Penetrometer

                  the 0-inch reading. Record readings in tabular fashion for engineer use similar to the formats
                  shown in appendix G.
                   If you suspect the cone is encountering stone or other foreign body at the depth where a

                        reading is desired, make another penetration nearby.
                   Take five penetrations at each location, using an X configuration in a 12-inch radius circle.

                        Note the readings at each 2-inch increment. Once an AI (15) has been reached, the follow-on
                        depths can be assigned an AI (15) after the following criteria have been satisfied:
                   A minimum of three sites must be tested to ensure that the lower depth has an AI (15) or
                        higher. These tests should be taken at least every 6 inches down to the 24-inch depth or lower
                        for the heaviest C-130 landing and highest pass levels if very low (4) AI values are suggested
                        to exist below the 24-inch depths. An auger may be used to penetrate to the desired depth. At
                        a minimum, one reading must be taken on the turnarounds, the parking apron, and off center
                        on the runway.
                   If a suspected abnormal layer is present, take enough readings to the 24-inch layer to verify
                        the extent of the area. Since this is the most critical area, the entire airfield must be evaluated
                        based on the abnormal layer.
              Step 4. After the readings at a site have been taken, the readings for each depth at the site are
                  averaged. The critical depth is then determined, and the critical AI is determined by averaging the
                  average readings in the critical depth. After critical AIs are determined for all sites, the lowest
                  critical AI is assigned for the entire airfield.
              Step 5. Determine the surface thickness requirements as described in chapter 4.

DETERMINATION OF CRITICAL AIRFIELD INDEX RUNWAYS AND
TAXIWAYS

     H-9. To determine the critical airfield index for runways and taxiways, use the following procedure:
              Step 1. The first reading will be taken 50 feet from one end of the runway or taxiway on the
                  centerline.
              Step 2. Follow-on readings will be read every 200 feet and will be staggered 20 feet off the
                  centerline. This means that if the second reading is 20 feet right of the centerline, the third reading
                  will be 20 feet left of the centerline, and so on.
              Step 3. This pattern will be repeated until the midpoint is reached. After the midpoint, the survey
                  team will go to the other end of the runway or taxiway, start the readings 50 feet on center from
                  that end, and repeat the survey pattern back to the midpoint.

PARKING APRONS

     H-10. To determine the critical airfield index for parking aprons, use the following procedure:
              Step 1. Locate the center of the area, and take the first set of readings.
              Step 2. Take the rest of the readings every 200 feet in both directions until the extent of the area
                  has been covered (figure G-3, page G-4).

HAMMERHEAD TURNAROUNDS

     H-11. To determine the critical airfield index for hammerhead turnarounds, use the following procedure:
              Step 1. Locate the ENTER area and take the set of readings.
              Step 2. Take the rest of the readings 40 feet in both directions until the extent of the area has been
                  covered. A minimum of one reading at the parking apron, runway, and hammerhead must be taken
                  to the 24-inch depth to ensure that the soil profile is accurate. If a soft layer is suspected or located
                  beneath a hard, upper layer, take enough readings down to the 24-inch depth to ensure complete
                  and accurate coverage of the area.

29 February 2016  TM 3-34.48-2  H-3

                  Glossary

The glossary lists acronyms and terms with Army or joint definitions. Where Army and
joint definitions differ, (Army) precedes the definition. Terms for which TM 3-34.48-
2 is the proponent are marked with an asterisk (*). The proponent publication for other
terms is listed in parentheses after the definition.

SECTION I – ACRONYMS AND ABBREVIATIONS

      AC          asphalt concrete
    ACN           aircraft classification number
  ADRP            Army doctrine reference publication
   AFCS           Army Facilities Components System
    AFH           Air Force handbook
      AFI         Air Force instruction
    AFM           Air Force manual
AFMAN             Air Force manual
AFPAM             Air Force pamphlet
     AFR          Air Force regulation
                  attack helicopter
      AH          airfield index
       AI         Alabama
       AL         assault landing zone
     ALZ          airfield marking patterns
    AMP           Army regulation
      AR          American Society for Testing and Materials
  ASTM            attention
      attn        American wire guage
   AWG            California Bearing Ratio
    CBR           Capabilities Development and Integration Directorate
   CDID           Code of Federal Regulations
     CFR          cargo helicopter
      CH          cone index
       CI         clay low plasticity
       CL         Concepts, Organizations, and Doctrine Development Division
CODDD             Department of the Army
      DA          District of Columbia
      DC          dynamic cone penetrometer
     DCP          Department of Defense
      DD          atmoshere pressure and altitude
       dH         Defense Logistics Agency
    DLA           Department of Defense
    DOD
